*** 5.20 How to customize indentation in C, C=++=, and Java buffers? [[#How-to-customize-indentation-in-C_002c-C_002b_002b_002c-and-Java-buffers_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-to-customize-indentation-in-C_002c-C_002b_002b_002c-and-Java-buffers_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The Emacs =cc-mode= features an interactive procedure for customizing
the indentation style, which is fully explained in the CC Mode manual
that is part of the Emacs distribution, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/ccmode.html#Customizing-Indentation][Customization Indentation]]
in The CC Mode Manual. Here's a short summary of the procedure:

1. Go to the beginning of the first line where you don't like the
   indentation and type C-c C-o. Emacs will prompt you for the syntactic
   symbol; type RET to accept the default it suggests.

2. Emacs now prompts for the offset of this syntactic symbol, showing
   the default (the current definition) inside parentheses. You can
   choose one of these:
   - =0= :: No extra indentation.

   - =+= :: Indent one basic offset.

   - =-= :: Outdent one basic offset.

   - =++= :: Indent two basic offsets

   - =--= :: Outdent two basic offsets.

   - =*= :: Indent half basic offset.

   - =/= :: Outdent half basic offset.

3. After choosing one of these symbols, type C-c C-q to reindent the
   line or the block according to what you just specified.

4. If you don't like the result, go back to step 1. Otherwise, add the
   following line to your init file (see
   [[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

   #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
   (c-set-offset 'syntactic-symbol offset)
   #+end_src

   where =syntactic-symbol= is the name Emacs shows in the minibuffer
   when you type C-c C-o at the beginning of the line, and =offset= is
   one of the indentation symbols listed above (=+=, =/=, =0=, etc.)
   that you've chosen during the interactive procedure.

5. Go to the next line whose indentation is not to your liking and
   repeat the process there.

It is recommended to put all the resulting =(c-set-offset ...)=
customizations inside a C mode hook, like this:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(defun my-c-mode-hook ()
  (c-set-offset ...)
  (c-set-offset ...))
(add-hook 'c-mode-hook 'my-c-mode-hook)
#+end_src

Using =c-mode-hook= avoids the need to put a =(require 'cc-mode)= into
your init file, because =c-set-offset= might be unavailable when
=cc-mode= is not loaded.

Note that =c-mode-hook= runs for C source files only; use
=c++-mode-hook= for C=++= sources, =java-mode-hook= for Java sources,
etc. If you want the same customizations to be in effect in /all/
languages supported by =cc-mode=, use =c-mode-common-hook=.

--------------

*** 5.21 How do I make Emacs “typeover” or “overwrite” instead of inserting? [[#How-do-I-make-Emacs-_0060_0060typeover_0027_0027-or-_0060_0060overwrite_0027_0027-instead-of-inserting_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-make-Emacs-_0060_0060typeover_0027_0027-or-_0060_0060overwrite_0027_0027-instead-of-inserting_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
M-x overwrite-mode (a minor mode). This toggles =overwrite-mode= on and
off, so exiting from =overwrite-mode= is as easy as another M-x
overwrite-mode.

On some systems, Insert toggles =overwrite-mode= on and off.

*** 5.22 How do I stop Emacs from beeping on a terminal? [[#How-do-I-stop-Emacs-from-beeping-on-a-terminal_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-stop-Emacs-from-beeping-on-a-terminal_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Martin R. Frank writes:

Tell Emacs to use the /visible bell/ instead of the audible bell, and
set the visible bell to nothing.

That is, put the following in your =TERMCAP= environment variable
(assuming you have one):

#+begin_src example-preformatted
... :vb=: ...
#+end_src

And evaluate the following Lisp form:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
(setq visible-bell t)
#+end_src

*** 5.23 How do I turn down the bell volume in Emacs running under X? [[#How-do-I-turn-down-the-bell-volume-in-Emacs-running-under-X_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-turn-down-the-bell-volume-in-Emacs-running-under-X_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
On X Window system, you can adjust the bell volume and duration for all
programs with the shell command =xset=.

Invoking =xset= without any arguments produces some basic information,
including the following:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
usage:  xset [-display host:dpy] option ...
  To turn bell off:
      -b                b off               b 0
  To set bell volume, pitch and duration:
       b [vol [pitch [dur]]]          b on
#+end_src

--------------

*** 5.24 How do I tell Emacs to automatically indent a new line to the indentation of the previous line? [[#How-do-I-tell-Emacs-to-automatically-indent-a-new-line-to-the-indentation-of-the-previous-line_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-tell-Emacs-to-automatically-indent-a-new-line-to-the-indentation-of-the-previous-line_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Such behavior is automatic (in Text mode). From the =etc/NEWS= file for
Emacs 20.2:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
,** In Text mode, now only blank lines separate paragraphs.  This makes
it possible to get the full benefit of Adaptive Fill mode in Text mode,
and other modes derived from it (such as Mail mode).  TAB in Text
mode now runs the command indent-relative; this makes a practical
difference only when you use indented paragraphs.

If you want spaces at the beginning of a line to start a paragraph, use
the new mode, Paragraph Indent Text mode.
#+end_src

If you have =auto-fill-mode= turned on (see
[[#Turning-on-auto_002dfill-by-default][How do I turn on =auto-fill-mode= by default?]]),
you can tell Emacs to prefix every line with a certain character
sequence, the /fill prefix/. Type the prefix at the beginning of a line,
position point after it, and then type C-x . (=set-fill-prefix=) to set
the fill prefix. Thereafter, auto-filling will automatically put the
fill prefix at the beginning of new lines, and M-q (=fill-paragraph=)
will maintain any fill prefix when refilling the paragraph.

If you have paragraphs with different levels of indentation, you will
have to set the fill prefix to the correct value each time you move to a
new paragraph. There are many packages available to deal with this (see
[[#Packages-that-do-not-come-with-Emacs][Where can I get Emacs Lisp packages that don't come with Emacs?]]).
Look for “fill” and “indent” keywords for guidance.

--------------

*** 5.25 How do I show which parenthesis matches the one I'm looking at? [[#How-do-I-show-which-parenthesis-matches-the-one-I_0027m-looking-at_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-show-which-parenthesis-matches-the-one-I_0027m-looking-at_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
By default, =show-paren-mode= is enabled in all editing buffers.

Alternatives to this mode include:

- If you're looking at a right parenthesis (or brace or bracket) you can
  delete it and reinsert it. Emacs will momentarily move the cursor to
  the matching parenthesis.
- C-M-f (=forward-sexp=) and C-M-b (=backward-sexp=) will skip over one
  set of balanced parentheses, so you can see which parentheses match.
  (You can train it to skip over balanced brackets and braces at the
  same time by modifying the syntax table.)
-  Here is some Emacs Lisp that will make the % key show the matching
  parenthesis, like in =vi=. In addition, if the cursor isn't over a
  parenthesis, it simply inserts a % like normal.

  #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
  ;; By an unknown contributor

  (keymap-global-set "%" 'match-paren)

  (defun match-paren (arg)
    "Go to the matching paren if on a paren; otherwise insert %."
    (interactive "p")
    (cond ((looking-at "\\s(") (forward-list 1) (backward-char 1))
          ((looking-at "\\s)") (forward-char 1) (backward-list 1))
          (t (self-insert-command (or arg 1)))))
  #+end_src

--------------

<<Hiding-_0023ifdef-lines>>

*** 5.27 How do I repeat a command as many times as possible? [[#How-do-I-repeat-a-command-as-many-times-as-possible_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-repeat-a-command-as-many-times-as-possible_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Use the =repeat= command (C-x z) to repeat the last command. If you
preface it with a prefix argument, the prefix arg is applied to the
command.

You can also type C-x ESC ESC (=repeat-complex-command=) to reinvoke
commands that used the minibuffer to get arguments. In
=repeat-complex-command= you can type M-p and M-n (and also up-arrow and
down-arrow, if your keyboard has these keys) to scan through all the
different complex commands you've typed.

To repeat a set of commands, use keyboard macros. Use C-x ( and C-x ) to
make a keyboard macro that invokes the command and then type C-x e. See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Keyboard-Macros][Keyboard Macros]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

If you're really desperate for the =.= command in =vi= that redoes the
last insertion/deletion, use VIPER, a =vi= emulation mode which comes
with Emacs, and which appears to support it.

--------------

:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: What-are-the-valid-X-resource-settings-_0028i_002ee_002e_002c-stuff-in-_002eXdefaults_0029_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#X-Resources][X Resources]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

You can also use a resource editor, such as editres (for X11R5 and
onwards), to look at the resource names for the menu bar, assuming Emacs
was compiled with the X toolkit.

*** 5.29 How do I execute (“evaluate”) a piece of Emacs Lisp code? [[#How-do-I-execute-_0028_0060_0060evaluate_0027_0027_0029-a-piece-of-Emacs-Lisp-code_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-execute-_0028_0060_0060evaluate_0027_0027_0029-a-piece-of-Emacs-Lisp-code_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
There are a number of ways to execute (/evaluate/, in Lisp lingo) an
Emacs Lisp /form/:

- If you want it evaluated every time you run Emacs, put it in a file
  named =.emacs.d/init.el= in your home directory. This is known as
  “your init file,” and contains all of your personal customizations
  (see
  [[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]).

- You can type the form in the =*scratch*= buffer, and then type LFD (or
  C-j) after it. The result of evaluating the form will be inserted in
  the buffer.

- In =emacs-lisp-mode=, typing C-M-x evaluates a top-level form before
  or around point.

- Typing C-x C-e in any buffer evaluates the Lisp form immediately
  before point and prints its value in the echo area.

- Typing M-: or M-x eval-expression allows you to type a Lisp form in
  the minibuffer which will be evaluated once you press RET.

- You can use M-x load-file to have Emacs evaluate all the Lisp forms in
  a file. (To do this from Lisp use the function =load= instead.)

  The functions =load-library=, =eval-region=, =eval-buffer=, =require=,
  and =autoload= are also useful; see
  [[#Emacs-Lisp-documentation][Where can I get documentation on Emacs Lisp?]],
  if you want to learn more about them.

--------------

<<Changing-the-length-of-a-Tab>>

*** 5.30 How do I change Emacs's idea of the TAB character's length? [[#How-do-I-change-Emacs_0027s-idea-of-the-TAB-character_0027s-length_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-change-Emacs_0027s-idea-of-the-TAB-character_0027s-length_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Set the default value of the variable =tab-width=. For example, to set
TAB stops every 10 characters, insert the following in your init file
(see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq-default tab-width 10)
#+end_src

Do not confuse variable =tab-width= with variable =tab-stop-list=. The
former is used for the display of literal TAB characters. The latter
controls what characters are inserted when you press the TAB character
in certain modes.

--------------

*** 5.31 How do I insert <some text> at the beginning of every line? [[#How-do-I-insert-_003csome-text_003e-at-the-beginning-of-every-line_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-insert-_003csome-text_003e-at-the-beginning-of-every-line_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
To do this to an entire buffer, type M-< M-x replace-regexp RET ^ RET
your text RET.

To do this to a region, use =string-insert-rectangle=. Set the mark
(C-SPC) at the beginning of the first line you want to prefix, move the
cursor to last line to be prefixed, and type M-x string-insert-rectangle
RET. To do this for the whole buffer, type C-x h M-x
string-insert-rectangle RET.

If you are trying to prefix a yanked mail message with ‘=>=', you might
want to set the variable =mail-yank-prefix=. In Message buffers, you can
even use M-; to cite yanked messages (M-; runs the function
=comment-region=, it is a general-purpose mechanism to comment regions)
(see
[[#Changing-the-included-text-prefix][How do I change the included text prefix in mail/news followups?]]).

--------------

<<Forcing-the-cursor-to-remain-in-the-same-column>>

*** 5.32 How do I make Emacs behave like this: when I go up or down, the cursor should stay in the same column even if the line is too short? [[#How-do-I-make-Emacs-behave-like-this_003a-when-I-go-up-or-down_002c-the-cursor-should-stay-in-the-same-column-even-if-the-line-is-too-short_003f][¶]]

:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-make-Emacs-behave-like-this_003a-when-I-go-up-or-down_002c-the-cursor-should-stay-in-the-same-column-even-if-the-line-is-too-short_003f
:CLASS: section

See also the variable =track-eol= and the command =set-goal-column=
bound to C-x C-n (see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Moving-Point][Moving Point]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual).

*** 5.34 How do I use regexps (regular expressions) in Emacs? [[#How-do-I-use-regexps-_0028regular-expressions_0029-in-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-use-regexps-_0028regular-expressions_0029-in-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Regexp-Backslash][Regexp Backslash]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

The =or= operator is ‘=\|=', not ‘=|=', and the grouping operators are
‘=\(=' and ‘=\)='. Also, the string syntax for a backslash is ‘=\\='. To
specify a regular expression like ‘=xxx\(foo\|bar\)=' in a Lisp string,
use ‘=xxx\\(foo\\|bar\\)='.

Note the doubled backslashes!

- Unlike in Unix =grep=, =sed=, etc., a complement character set
  (‘=[^...]=') can match a newline character (LFD a.k.a. C-j a.k.a.
  ‘=\n='), unless newline is mentioned as one of the characters not to
  match.
- The character syntax regexps (e.g., ‘=\sw=') are not meaningful inside
  character set regexps (e.g., ‘=[aeiou]='). (This is actually typical
  for regexp syntax.)

--------------

*** 5.35 How do I perform a replace operation across more than one file? [[#How-do-I-perform-a-replace-operation-across-more-than-one-file_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-perform-a-replace-operation-across-more-than-one-file_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Dired mode (M-x dired RET, or C-x d) supports the command
=dired-do-find-regexp-and-replace= (Q), which allows users to replace
regular expressions in multiple files.

You can use this command to perform search/replace operations on
multiple files by following the following steps:

- Assemble a list of files you want to operate on with either
  =find-dired=, =find-name-dired= or =find-grep-dired=.
- Mark all files in the resulting Dired buffer using t.
- Use Q to start a =query-replace-regexp= session on the marked files.
- To accept all replacements in each file, hit !.

Another way to do the same thing is to use the “tags” feature of Emacs:
it includes the command =tags-query-replace= which performs a
query-replace across all the files mentioned in the =TAGS= file. See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Identifier-Search][Identifier Search]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

--------------

<<Documentation-for-etags>>

[[#Replacing-text-across-multiple-files][How do I perform a replace operation across more than one file?]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

:CUSTOM_ID: Where-is-the-documentation-for-etags_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The =etags= man page should be in the same place as the =emacs= man
page.

Quick command-line switch descriptions are also available. For example,
‘=etags -H='.

*** 5.37 How do I disable backup files? [[#How-do-I-disable-backup-files_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-disable-backup-files_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
You probably don't want to do this, since backups are useful, especially
when something goes wrong.

To avoid seeing backup files (and other “uninteresting” files) in Dired,
load =dired-x= by adding the following to your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(with-eval-after-load 'dired
  (require 'dired-x))
#+end_src

With =dired-x= loaded, C-x M-o toggles omitting in each dired buffer.
You can make omitting the default for new dired buffers by putting the
following in your init file:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(add-hook 'dired-mode-hook 'dired-omit-mode)
#+end_src

If you're tired of seeing backup files whenever you do an ‘=ls=' at the
Unix shell, try GNU =ls= with the ‘=-B=' option. GNU =ls= is part of the
GNU Fileutils package, available from [[https://ftp.gnu.org]] and its
mirrors (see
[[#Current-GNU-distributions][Where can I get other up-to-date GNU stuff?]]).

To disable or change the way backups are made, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Backup-Names][Backup Names]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

You can control where Emacs puts backup files by customizing the
variable =backup-directory-alist=. This variable's value specifies that
files whose names match specific patterns should have their backups put
in certain directories. A typical use is to add the element
=("." . ==dir==)= to force Emacs to put *all* backup files in the
directory =dir=.

--------------

<<Disabling-auto_002dsave_002dmode>>

Next:
[[#Not-writing-files-to-the-current-directory][Making Emacs write all auxiliary files somewhere else]],
Previous: [[#Disabling-backups][How do I disable backup files?]], Up:
[[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.38 How do I disable =auto-save-mode=? [[#How-do-I-disable-auto_002dsave_002dmode_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-disable-auto_002dsave_002dmode_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
You probably don't want to do this, since auto-saving is useful,
especially when Emacs or your computer crashes while you are editing a
document.

Instead, you might want to change the variable =auto-save-interval=,
which specifies how many keystrokes Emacs waits before auto-saving.
Increasing this value forces Emacs to wait longer between auto-saves,
which might annoy you less.

You might also want to look into Sebastian Kremer's =auto-save= package
(see
[[#Packages-that-do-not-come-with-Emacs][Where can I get Emacs Lisp packages that don't come with Emacs?]]).
This package also allows you to place all auto-save files in one
directory, such as =/tmp=.

To disable or change how =auto-save-mode= works, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Auto-Save][Auto Save]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

--------------

<<Not-writing-files-to-the-current-directory>>

Next:
[[#Going-to-a-line-by-number][How can I go to a certain line given its number?]],
Previous:
[[#Disabling-auto_002dsave_002dmode][How do I disable =auto-save-mode=?]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.39 Making Emacs write all auxiliary files somewhere else [[#Making-Emacs-write-all-auxiliary-files-somewhere-else][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Making-Emacs-write-all-auxiliary-files-somewhere-else
:CLASS: section
:END:
By default, Emacs may create many new files in the directory where
you're editing a file. If you're editing the file =/home/user/foo.txt=,
Emacs will create the lock file =/home/user/.#foo.txt=, the auto-save
file =/home/user/#foo.txt#=, and when you save the file, Emacs will
create the backup file =/home/user/foo.txt~=. (The first two files are
deleted when you save the file.)

This may be inconvenient in some setups, so Emacs has mechanisms for
changing the locations of all these files.

- =auto-save-file-name-transforms= :: (see
  [[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/elisp.html#Auto_002dSaving][Auto-Saving]]
  in GNU Emacs Lisp Reference Manual).

- =lock-file-name-transforms= :: (see
  [[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/elisp.html#File-Locks][File Locks]]
  in GNU Emacs Lisp Reference Manual).

- =backup-directory-alist= :: (see
  [[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/elisp.html#Making-Backups][Making Backups]]
  in GNU Emacs Lisp Reference Manual).

For instance, to write all these things to =~/.emacs.d/aux/=:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq lock-file-name-transforms
      '(("\\`/.*/\\([^/]+\\)\\'" "~/.emacs.d/aux/\\1" t)))
(setq auto-save-file-name-transforms
      '(("\\`/.*/\\([^/]+\\)\\'" "~/.emacs.d/aux/\\1" t)))
(setq backup-directory-alist
      '((".*" . "~/.emacs.d/aux/")))
#+end_src

--------------

<<Going-to-a-line-by-number>>

Next:
[[#Modifying-pull_002ddown-menus][How can I create or modify new pull-down menu options?]],
Previous:
[[#Not-writing-files-to-the-current-directory][Making Emacs write all auxiliary files somewhere else]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.40 How can I go to a certain line given its number? [[#How-can-I-go-to-a-certain-line-given-its-number_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-go-to-a-certain-line-given-its-number_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Are you sure you indeed need to go to a line by its number? Perhaps all
you want is to display a line in your source file for which a compiler
printed an error message? If so, compiling from within Emacs using the
M-x compile and M-x recompile commands is a much more effective way of
doing that. Emacs automatically intercepts the compile error messages,
inserts them into a special buffer called =*compilation*=, and lets you
visit the locus of each message in the source. Type C-x ` to step
through the offending lines one by one (you can also use M-g M-p and M-g
M-n to go to the previous and next matches directly). Click mouse-2 or
press RET on a message text in the =*compilation*= buffer to go to the
line whose number is mentioned in that message.

But if you indeed need to go to a certain text line, type M-g M-g (which
is the default binding of the =goto-line= function). Emacs will prompt
you for the number of the line and go to that line.

You can do this faster by invoking =goto-line= with a numeric argument
that is the line's number. For example, C-u 286 M-g M-g will jump to
line number 286 in the current buffer.

--------------

<<Modifying-pull_002ddown-menus>>

Next:
[[#Deleting-menus-and-menu-options][How do I delete menus and menu options?]],
Previous:
[[#Going-to-a-line-by-number][How can I go to a certain line given its number?]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.41 How can I create or modify new pull-down menu options? [[#How-can-I-create-or-modify-new-pull_002ddown-menu-options_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-create-or-modify-new-pull_002ddown-menu-options_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Each menu title (e.g., ‘=File=', ‘=Edit=', ‘=Buffers=') represents a
local or global keymap. Selecting a menu title with the mouse displays
that keymap's non-=nil= contents in the form of a menu.

So to add a menu option to an existing menu, all you have to do is add a
new definition to the appropriate keymap. Adding a ‘=Forward Word=' item
to the ‘=Edit=' menu thus requires the following Lisp code:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set global-map
  "<menu-bar> <edit> <forward>"
  '("Forward word" . forward-word))
#+end_src

The first line adds the entry to the global keymap, which includes
global menu bar entries. Replacing the reference to =global-map= with a
local keymap would add this menu option only within a particular mode.

The second line describes the path from the menu-bar to the new entry.
Placing this menu entry underneath the ‘=File=' menu would mean changing
the word =edit= in the second line to =file=.

The third line is a cons cell whose first element is the title that will
be displayed, and whose second element is the function that will be
called when that menu option is invoked.

To add a new menu, rather than a new option to an existing menu, we must
define an entirely new keymap:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set global-map "<menu-bar> <words>"
  (cons "Words" (make-sparse-keymap "Words")))
#+end_src

The above code creates a new sparse keymap, gives it the name ‘=Words=',
and attaches it to the global menu bar. Adding the ‘=Forward Word=' item
to this new menu would thus require the following code:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set global-map
  "<menu-bar> <words> <forward>"
  '("Forward word" . forward-word))
#+end_src

Note that because of the way keymaps work, menu options are displayed
with the more recently defined items at the top. Thus if you were to
define menu options ‘=foo=', ‘=bar=', and ‘=baz=' (in that order), the
menu option ‘=baz=' would appear at the top, and ‘=foo=' would be at the
bottom.

One way to avoid this problem is to use the function =keymap-set-after=,
which works the same as =keymap-set=, but lets you modify where items
appear. The following Lisp code would insert the ‘=Forward Word=' item
in the ‘=Edit=' menu immediately following the ‘=Undo=' item:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set-after
  (keymap-lookup global-map "<menu-bar> <edit>")
  "<forward>"
  '("Forward word" . forward-word)
  'undo)
#+end_src

Note how the second and third arguments to =keymap-set-after= are
different from those of =keymap-set=, and that we have added a new
(final) argument, the function after which our new key should be
defined.

To move a menu option from one position to another, simply evaluate
=keymap-set-after= with the appropriate final argument.

More detailed information---and more examples of how to create and
modify menu options---are in the Emacs Lisp Reference Manual, under
“Menu Keymaps.” (See
[[#Emacs-Lisp-documentation][Where can I get documentation on Emacs Lisp?]],
for information on this manual.)

--------------

<<Deleting-menus-and-menu-options>>

*** 5.42 How do I delete menus and menu options? [[#How-do-I-delete-menus-and-menu-options_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-delete-menus-and-menu-options_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The simplest way to remove a menu is to set its keymap to ‘=nil='. For
example, to delete the ‘=Words=' menu (see
[[#Modifying-pull_002ddown-menus][How can I create or modify new pull-down menu options?]]),
use:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set global-map "<menu-bar> <words>" nil)
#+end_src

Similarly, removing a menu option requires redefining a keymap entry to
=nil=. For example, to delete the ‘=Forward word=' menu option from the
‘=Edit=' menu (we added it in
[[#Modifying-pull_002ddown-menus][How can I create or modify new pull-down menu options?]]),
use:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-set global-map "<menu-bar> <edit> <forward>" nil)
#+end_src

--------------

<<Turning-on-syntax-highlighting>>

Next:
[[#Scrolling-only-one-line][How can I force Emacs to scroll only one line when I move past the bottom of the screen?]],

[[#Deleting-menus-and-menu-options][How do I delete menus and menu options?]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.43 How do I turn on syntax highlighting? [[#How-do-I-turn-on-syntax-highlighting_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-turn-on-syntax-highlighting_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
=font-lock-mode= is the standard way to have Emacs perform syntax
highlighting in the current buffer. It is enabled by default.

With =font-lock-mode= turned on, different types of text will appear in
different colors. For instance, in a programming mode, variables will
appear in one face, keywords in a second, and comments in a third.

To turn =font-lock-mode= off within an existing buffer, use M-x
font-lock-mode RET.

Highlighting a buffer with =font-lock-mode= can take quite a while, and
cause an annoying delay in display, so several features exist to work
around this.

Turning on =font-lock-mode= automatically activates /Just-In-Time
fontification/ provided by =jit-lock-mode=. =jit-lock-mode= defers the
fontification of portions of buffer until you actually need to see them,
and can also fontify while Emacs is idle. This makes display of the
visible portion of a buffer almost instantaneous. For details about
customizing =jit-lock-mode=, type C-h f jit-lock-mode RET.

Different levels of decoration are available, from slight to gaudy. More
decoration means you need to wait more time for a buffer to be fontified
(or a faster machine). To control how decorated your buffers should
become, set the value of =font-lock-maximum-decoration= in your init
file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]),
with a =nil= value indicating default (usually minimum) decoration, and
a =t= value indicating the maximum decoration. For the gaudiest possible
look, then, include the line

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq font-lock-maximum-decoration t)
#+end_src

in your init file. You can also set this variable such that different
modes are highlighted in a different ways; for more information, see the
documentation for =font-lock-maximum-decoration= with C-h v (or M-x
describe-variable RET).

Also see the documentation for the function =font-lock-mode=, available
by typing C-h f font-lock-mode (M-x describe-function RET font-lock-mode
RET).

To print buffers with the faces (i.e., colors and fonts) intact, use M-x
ps-print-buffer-with-faces or M-x ps-print-region-with-faces. You will
need a way to send text to a PostScript printer, or a PostScript
interpreter such as Ghostscript; consult the documentation of the
variables =ps-printer-name=, =ps-lpr-command=, and =ps-lpr-switches= for
more details.

--------------

<<Scrolling-only-one-line>>

[[#Turning-on-syntax-highlighting][How do I turn on syntax highlighting?]],
Up: [[#Common-requests][Common requests]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 5.44 How can I force Emacs to scroll only one line when I move past the bottom of the screen? [[#How-can-I-force-Emacs-to-scroll-only-one-line-when-I-move-past-the-bottom-of-the-screen_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-force-Emacs-to-scroll-only-one-line-when-I-move-past-the-bottom-of-the-screen_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Customize the =scroll-conservatively= variable with M-x
customize-variable RET scroll-conservatively RET and set it to a large
value like, say, 10000. For an explanation of what this means, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Auto-Scrolling][Auto Scrolling]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

Alternatively, use the following Lisp form in your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq scroll-conservatively most-positive-fixnum)
#+end_src

--------------

<<Editing-Windows-files>>

*** 5.45 How can I edit Windows files using Emacs? [[#How-can-I-edit-Windows-files-using-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-edit-Windows-files-using-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Detection and handling of Windows (and MS-DOS) files is performed
transparently. You can open Windows files on a Unix system, edit it, and
save it without having to worry about the file format.

When editing a Windows style file, the mode line will indicate that it
is a Windows file. On GNU/Linux, Unix and macOS systems, the string
‘=(DOS)=' will appear near the left edge of the mode line; on Windows
and MS-DOS, where the DOS end-of-line (EOL) format is the default, a
backslash (‘=\=') will appear in the mode line.

--------------

<<Filling-paragraphs-with-a-single-space>>

*** 5.46 How can I tell Emacs to fill paragraphs with a single space after each period? [[#How-can-I-tell-Emacs-to-fill-paragraphs-with-a-single-space-after-each-period_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-tell-Emacs-to-fill-paragraphs-with-a-single-space-after-each-period_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Add the following line to your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq sentence-end-double-space nil)
#+end_src

*** 5.47 Why these strange escape sequences from =ls= from the Shell mode? [[#Why-these-strange-escape-sequences-from-ls-from-the-Shell-mode_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-these-strange-escape-sequences-from-ls-from-the-Shell-mode_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
In many systems, =ls= is aliased to ‘=ls --color=', which prints using
ANSI color escape sequences. Emacs includes the =ansi-color= package,
which lets Shell mode recognize these escape sequences. It is enabled by
default.

--------------

*** 5.48 How can I start Emacs in full screen? [[#How-can-I-start-Emacs-in-full-screen_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-start-Emacs-in-full-screen_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Run Emacs with the ‘=--maximized=' command-line option or put the
following form at the top of your early init file (see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Early-Init-File][Early Init File]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual).

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(push '(fullscreen . maximized) default-frame-alist)
#+end_src

Note that while some customizations of =default-frame-alist= could have
undesirable effects when modified in the early init file, it is okay to
do it in this particular case. Adding it to the top of your normal init
file will also work, but leads to a visible resizing of the window that
some find distracting.

--------------

<<Emacs-in-a-Linux-console>>

*** 5.49 How can I alleviate the limitations of the Linux console? [[#How-can-I-alleviate-the-limitations-of-the-Linux-console_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-can-I-alleviate-the-limitations-of-the-Linux-console_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
If possible, we recommend running Emacs inside =fbterm=, when in a Linux
console. This brings the Linux console on par with most terminal
emulators under X. To do this, install =fbterm=, for example with the
package manager of your GNU/Linux distribution, and execute the command

#+begin_src example-preformatted
$ fbterm
#+end_src

This will create a sample configuration file =~/.fbtermrc= in your home
directory. Edit that file and change the options =font-names= and
=font-size= if necessary. For the former, you can choose one or more of
the lines in the output of the following command, separated by commas:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
$ fc-list :spacing=mono family | sed 's/ /\\ /g'
#+end_src

Note that you can fine-tune the appearance of the fonts by adding
attribute-value pairs, separated by colons, after each font name. For
example,

#+begin_src example-preformatted
font-names=DejaVu\ Sans\ Mono:style=bold:antialias=false
#+end_src

selects the bold style of the DejaVu Sans Mono font, and disables
anti-aliasing.

You can now start Emacs inside =fbterm= with the command

#+begin_src example-preformatted
$ fbterm -- env TERM=fbterm emacs
#+end_src

In some versions of =fbterm=, setting =TERM= to ‘=fbterm=' can be
omitted. To check whether it is needed, start Emacs inside =fbterm= with
the command

#+begin_src example-preformatted
$ fbterm -- emacs
#+end_src

and type M-x list-colors-display. If only 8 colors are displayed, it is
necessary; if 256 colors are displayed, it isn't.

You may want to add an alias for that command in your shell
configuration file. For example, if you use Bash, you can add the
following line to your =~/.bashrc= file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
alias emacs="fbterm -- env TERM=fbterm emacs"
#+end_src

or, if you use Emacs both in the Linux console and under X:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
[[ "$(tty)" =~ "/dev/tty" ]] && alias emacs="fbterm -- env TERM=fbterm emacs"
#+end_src

The =fbterm= terminal emulator may define a number of key bindings for
its own use, some of which conflict with those that Emacs uses. Execute
the following two commands as root to ensure that =fbterm= does not
define these key bindings:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
# chmod a-s `which fbterm`
# setcap cap_sys_tty_config=-ep `which fbterm`
#+end_src

If you use Emacs as root, the above is not enough however, because the
root user has all privileges. You can use the following command to start
Emacs inside =fbterm= as root while ensuring that =fbterm= does not
define any key bindings for its own use:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
# capsh --drop=cap_sys_tty_config -- -c "fbterm -- env TERM=fbterm emacs"
#+end_src

Again you may want to add a shortcut for that command in the shell
configuration file of the root user. In this case however, it is not
possible to use an alias, because the command line arguments passed to
Emacs need to be inserted in the string at the end of the command. A
wrapper script or a function can be used to do that. For example, if you
use Bash, you can add the following function in the root user
=~/.bashrc= file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
function emacs ()
{
  CMD="fbterm -- env TERM=fbterm emacs "
  for ARG in "$@"
  do
    CMD="$CMD '$ARG' "
  done
  capsh --drop=cap_sys_tty_config -- -c "$CMD"
}
#+end_src

--------------

** 6 Bugs and problems [[#Bugs-and-problems-1][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Bugs-and-problems-1
:CLASS: chapter
:END:
The Emacs manual lists some common kinds of trouble users could get
into, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Lossage][Dealing with Emacs Trouble]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual, so you might look there if the problem you
encounter isn't described in this chapter. If you decide you've
discovered a bug, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Bugs][Reporting Bugs]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual, for instructions how to do that.

The file =etc/PROBLEMS= in the Emacs distribution lists various known
problems with building and using Emacs on specific platforms; type C-h
C-p to read it.

- [[#Problems-with-very-large-files][Does Emacs have problems with large files?]]
- [[#g_t_005eM-in-the-shell-buffer][How do I get rid of ‘=^M=' or echoed commands in my shell buffer?]]
- [[#Problems-with-Shell-Mode][Why do I get an error message when I try to run M-x shell?]]
- [[#Termcap_002fTerminfo-entries-for-Emacs][Where is the termcap/terminfo entry for terminal type ‘=emacs='?]]
- [[#Errors-with-init-files][Why does Emacs say ‘=Error in init file='?]]
- [[#Emacs-ignores-X-resources][Why does Emacs ignore my X resources (my .Xdefaults file)?]]
- [[#Emacs-ignores-frame-parameters][Why don't my customizations of the frame parameters work?]]
- [[#Editing-files-with-_0024-in-the-name][How do I edit a file with a ‘=$=' in its name?]]
- [[#Shell-mode-loses-the-current-directory][Why does shell mode lose track of the shell's current directory?]]
- [[#Security-risks-with-Emacs][Are there any security risks in Emacs?]]
- [[#Dired-claims-that-no-file-is-on-this-line][Dired says, ‘=no file on this line=' when I try to do something.]]

<<Problems-with-very-large-files>>

*** 6.1 Does Emacs have problems with large files? [[#Does-Emacs-have-problems-with-large-files_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Does-Emacs-have-problems-with-large-files_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Emacs has an inherent fixed limitation on the size of buffers. This
limit is stricter than the maximum size of objects supported by other
programs on the same architecture.

The maximum buffer size on 64-bit machines is 2.3 exabytes
(=most-positive-fixnum=).

Emacs compiled on a 32-bit machine can handle buffers up to 512 MBytes.
If Emacs was built using the =--with-wide-int= flag, the maximum buffer
size on 32-bit machines is 2 GB.

Due to things like decoding of multibyte characters, you can only visit
files with a size that is roughly half the buffer size limit. When
visiting compressed archives, the file size limit will be smaller than
that due to decompression.

--------------

<<g_t_005eM-in-the-shell-buffer>>

Next:
[[#Problems-with-Shell-Mode][Why do I get an error message when I try to run M-x shell?]],
Previous:
[[#Problems-with-very-large-files][Does Emacs have problems with large files?]],
Up: [[#Bugs-and-problems][Bugs and problems]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 6.2 How do I get rid of ‘=^M=' or echoed commands in my shell buffer? [[#How-do-I-get-rid-of-_005eM-or-echoed-commands-in-my-shell-buffer_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-get-rid-of-_005eM-or-echoed-commands-in-my-shell-buffer_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Try typing M-x comint-strip-ctrl-m RET while in =shell-mode= to make
them go away. If that doesn't work, you have several options:

For =tcsh=, put this in your =.cshrc= (or =.tcshrc=) file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
if ($?INSIDE_EMACS && $?tcsh)
    unset edit
    stty -icrnl -onlcr -echo susp ^Z
endif
#+end_src

Or put this in your =.emacs_tcsh= or =~/.emacs.d/init_tcsh.sh= file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
unset edit
stty -icrnl -onlcr -echo susp ^Z
#+end_src

Alternatively, use =csh= in your shell buffers instead of =tcsh=. One
way is:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setq explicit-shell-file-name "/bin/csh")
#+end_src

and another is to do this in your =.cshrc= (or =.tcshrc=) file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
setenv ESHELL /bin/csh
#+end_src

(You must start Emacs over again with the environment variable properly
set for this to take effect.)

You can also set the =ESHELL= environment variable in Emacs Lisp with
the following Lisp form,

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(setenv "ESHELL" "/bin/csh")
#+end_src

The above solutions try to prevent the shell from producing the ‘=^M='
characters in the first place. If this is not possible (e.g., if you use
a Windows shell), you can get Emacs to remove these characters from the
buffer by adding this to your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(add-hook 'comint-output-filter-functions #'comint-strip-ctrl-m)
#+end_src

On a related note: if your shell is echoing your input line in the shell
buffer, you might want to customize the =comint-process-echoes= variable
in your shell buffers, or try the following command in your shell
start-up file:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
stty -icrnl -onlcr -echo susp ^Z
#+end_src

--------------

<<Problems-with-Shell-Mode>>

[[#Termcap_002fTerminfo-entries-for-Emacs][Where is the termcap/terminfo entry for terminal type ‘=emacs='?]],

*** 6.3 Why do I get an error message when I try to run M-x shell? [[#Why-do-I-get-an-error-message-when-I-try-to-run-M_002dx-shell_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-do-I-get-an-error-message-when-I-try-to-run-M_002dx-shell_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
This might happen because Emacs tries to look for the shell in a wrong
place. If you know where your shell executable is, set the variable
=explicit-shell-file-name= in your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]])
to point to its full file name.

Some people have trouble with Shell Mode on MS-Windows because of
intrusive antivirus software; disabling the resident antivirus program
solves the problems in those cases.

--------------

<<Termcap_002fTerminfo-entries-for-Emacs>>

[[#Errors-with-init-files][Why does Emacs say ‘=Error in init file='?]],

[[#Problems-with-Shell-Mode][Why do I get an error message when I try to run M-x shell?]],

[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 6.4 Where is the termcap/terminfo entry for terminal type ‘=emacs='? [[#Where-is-the-termcap_002fterminfo-entry-for-terminal-type-emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Where-is-the-termcap_002fterminfo-entry-for-terminal-type-emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The termcap entry for terminal type ‘=emacs=' is ordinarily put in the
‘=TERMCAP=' environment variable of subshells. It may help in certain
situations (e.g., using rlogin from shell buffer) to add an entry for
‘=emacs=' to the system-wide termcap file. Here is a correct termcap
entry for ‘=emacs=':

#+begin_src example-preformatted
emacs:tc=unknown:
#+end_src

To make a terminfo entry for ‘=emacs=', use =tic= or =captoinfo=. You
need to generate =/usr/lib/terminfo/e/emacs=. It may work to simply copy
=/usr/lib/terminfo/d/dumb= to =/usr/lib/terminfo/e/emacs=.

Having a termcap/terminfo entry will not enable the use of full screen
programs in shell buffers. Use M-x term for that instead.

A workaround to the problem of missing termcap/terminfo entries is to
change terminal type ‘=emacs=' to type ‘=dumb=' or ‘=unknown=' in your
shell start up file. =csh= users could put this in their =.cshrc= files:

#+begin_src example-preformatted
if ("$term" == emacs) set term=dumb
#+end_src

--------------

<<Errors-with-init-files>>

*** 6.5 Why does Emacs say ‘=Error in init file='? [[#Why-does-Emacs-say-Error-in-init-file_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-does-Emacs-say-Error-in-init-file_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
An error occurred while loading either your init file or the system-wide
file =site-lisp/default.el=. Emacs pops the =*Messages*= buffer, and
puts there some additional information about the error, to provide some
hints for debugging.

For information on how to debug your init file, see
[[#Debugging-a-customization-file][How do I debug an init file?]].

It may be the case that you need to load some package first, or use a
hook that will be evaluated after the package is loaded. A common case
of this is explained in
[[#Terminal-setup-code-works-after-Emacs-has-begun][Why doesn't this [terminal or window-system setup] code work in my init file, but it works just fine after Emacs starts up?]].

--------------

<<Emacs-ignores-X-resources>>

Next:
[[#Emacs-ignores-frame-parameters][Why don't my customizations of the frame parameters work?]],
Previous:
[[#Errors-with-init-files][Why does Emacs say ‘=Error in init file='?]],
Up: [[#Bugs-and-problems][Bugs and problems]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 6.6 Why does Emacs ignore my X resources (my .Xdefaults file)? [[#Why-does-Emacs-ignore-my-X-resources-_0028my-_002eXdefaults-file_0029_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-does-Emacs-ignore-my-X-resources-_0028my-_002eXdefaults-file_0029_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Emacs searches for X resources in the files specified by the following
environment variables:

- =XFILESEARCHPATH=
- =XUSERFILESEARCHPATH=
- =XAPPLRESDIR=

This emulates the functionality provided by programs written using the
Xt toolkit.

=XFILESEARCHPATH= and =XUSERFILESEARCHPATH= should be a list of file
names separated by colons. =XAPPLRESDIR= should be a list of directories
separated by colons.

Emacs searches for X resources:

1. specified on the command line, with the ‘=-xrm RESOURCESTRING='
   option,
2. then in the value of the ‘=XENVIRONMENT=' environment variable,
   - or if that is unset, in the file named =~/.Xdefaults-==hostname= if
     it exists (where =hostname= is the name of the machine Emacs is
     running on),
3. then in the screen-specific and server-wide resource properties
   provided by the server,
   - or if those properties are unset, in the file named =~/.Xdefaults=
     if it exists,
4. then in the files listed in ‘=XUSERFILESEARCHPATH=',
   - or in files named =lang==/Emacs= in directories listed in
     ‘=XAPPLRESDIR=' (where =lang= is the value of the =LANG=
     environment variable), if the ‘=LANG=' environment variable is set,
   - or in files named Emacs in the directories listed in
     ‘=XAPPLRESDIR='
   - or in =~/==lang==/Emacs= (if the =LANG= environment variable is
     set),
   - or in =~/Emacs=,
5. then in the files listed in =XFILESEARCHPATH=.

--------------

<<Emacs-ignores-frame-parameters>>

*** 6.7 Why don't my customizations of the frame parameters work? [[#Why-don_0027t-my-customizations-of-the-frame-parameters-work_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-don_0027t-my-customizations-of-the-frame-parameters-work_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
This probably happens because you have set the frame parameters in the
variable =initial-frame-alist=. That variable holds parameters used only
for the first frame created when Emacs starts. To customize the
parameters of all frames, change the variable =default-frame-alist=
instead.

These two variables exist because many users customize the initial frame
in a special way. For example, you could determine the position and size
of the initial frame, but would like to control the geometry of the
other frames by individually positioning each one of them.

--------------

*** 6.8 How do I edit a file with a ‘=$=' in its name? [[#How-do-I-edit-a-file-with-a-_0024-in-its-name_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-edit-a-file-with-a-_0024-in-its-name_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
When entering a file name in the minibuffer, Emacs will attempt to
expand a ‘=$=' followed by a word as an environment variable. To
suppress this behavior, type $$ instead.

--------------

*** 6.9 Why does shell mode lose track of the shell's current directory? [[#Why-does-shell-mode-lose-track-of-the-shell_0027s-current-directory_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-does-shell-mode-lose-track-of-the-shell_0027s-current-directory_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Emacs has no way of knowing when the shell actually changes its
directory. This is an intrinsic limitation of Unix. So it tries to guess
by recognizing ‘=cd=' commands. If you type cd followed by directory
with a variable reference (cd $HOME/bin) or with a shell metacharacter
(cd ../lib*), Emacs will fail to correctly guess the shell's new current
directory. A huge variety of fixes and enhancements to shell mode for
this problem have been written to handle this problem (see
[[#Finding-a-package-with-particular-functionality][How do I find an Emacs Lisp package that does XXX?]]).

You can tell Emacs the shell's current directory with the command M-x
dirs.

--------------

*** 6.10 Are there any security risks in Emacs? [[#Are-there-any-security-risks-in-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Are-there-any-security-risks-in-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
- Third party packages.

  Any package you install into Emacs can run arbitrary code with the
  same privileges as the Emacs process itself. Be aware of this when you
  use the package system (for example, =M-x list-packages=) with third
  party archives. Use only third parties that you can trust!

- Using an out-of-date Emacs version.

  For security purposes, we recommend always using the latest officially
  released version of Emacs. Using old versions of Emacs might put your
  security at risk, as newer versions occasionally include important
  security fixes. Please review the Emacs release notes and the
  =etc/NEWS= file for details.

  Upgrading to the most recent version is particularly important if you
  use Emacs as a mail client, or to edit files that come from untrusted
  sources. You should be able to install the latest version of Emacs
  through your system's package manager, and it is always available at
  [[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/][the Emacs website]].

- The =file-local-variable= feature. (Yes, a risk, but easy to change.)

  There is an Emacs feature that allows the setting of local values for
  variables when editing a file by including specially formatted text
  near the end of the file. This feature also includes the ability to
  have arbitrary Emacs Lisp code evaluated when the file is visited.
  Obviously, there is a potential for Trojan horses to exploit this
  feature.

  Emacs has a list of local variables that are known to be safe to set.
  If a file tries to set any variable outside this list, it asks the
  user to confirm whether the variables should be set. You can also tell
  Emacs whether to allow the evaluation of Emacs Lisp code found at the
  bottom of files by setting the variable =enable-local-eval=.

  See
  [[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#File-Variables][File Variables]]
  in The GNU Emacs Manual.

- Browsing the web.

  Emacs relies on C libraries to parse images, and historically, many of
  these have had exploitable weaknesses. If you're browsing the web with
  the eww browser, it will usually download and display images using
  these libraries. If an image library has a weakness, it may be used by
  an attacker to gain access.

--------------

<<Dired-claims-that-no-file-is-on-this-line>>

*** 6.11 Dired says, ‘=no file on this line=' when I try to do something. [[#Dired-says_002c-no-file-on-this-line-when-I-try-to-do-something_002e][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Dired-says_002c-no-file-on-this-line-when-I-try-to-do-something_002e
:CLASS: section
:END:
Dired uses a regular expression to find the beginning of a file name. In
a long Unix-style directory listing (‘=ls -l='), the file name starts
after the date. The regexp has thus been written to look for the date.
By default, it should understand dates and times regardless of the
language, but if your directory listing has an unusual format, Dired may
get confused.

There are two approaches to solving this. The first one involves setting
things up so that ‘=ls -l=' outputs a more standard format. See your OS
manual for more information.

The second approach involves changing the regular expression used by
dired, =directory-listing-before-filename-regexp=.

--------------

*** 7.1 How do I install Emacs? [[#How-do-I-install-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-install-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
This answer is meant for users of Unix and Unix-like systems. Users of
other operating systems should see See
[[#Emacs-for-other-operating-systems][Where can I get Emacs for macOS, MS Windows, etc?]].

Most GNU/Linux distributions provide pre-built Emacs packages. If Emacs
is not installed already, you can install it by running (as root) a
command such as ‘=dnf install emacs=' (Red Hat and derivatives; use
‘=yum=' in older distributions) or ‘=apt-get install emacs=' (Debian and
derivatives).

If you want to compile Emacs yourself, read the file =INSTALL= in the
source distribution. In brief:

- First download the Emacs sources. See
  [[#Current-GNU-distributions][Where can I get other up-to-date GNU stuff?]],
  for a list of sites that make them available. On
  [[https://ftp.gnu.org]], the main GNU distribution site, sources are
  available as

  [[https://ftp.gnu.org/pub/gnu/emacs/emacs-VERSION.tar.xz]]

  (Replace ‘=VERSION=' with the relevant version number, e.g.,
  ‘=28.1='.)

- Next uncompress and extract the source files. This requires the =xz=
  and =tar= programs, which are standard utilities. If your system does
  not have them, these can also be downloaded from
  [[https://ftp.gnu.org]].

  GNU =tar= can uncompress and extract in a single-step:

  #+begin_src example-preformatted
  tar -axvf emacs-VERSION.tar.xz
  #+end_src

- At this point, the Emacs sources should be sitting in a directory
  called =emacs-VERSION=. On most common Unix and Unix-like systems, you
  should be able to compile Emacs with the following commands:

  #+begin_src example-preformatted
  cd emacs-VERSION
  ./configure         # configure Emacs for your particular system
  make                # use Makefile to build components, then Emacs
  #+end_src

  If the =make= completes successfully, you can go on to install it.
  (See
  [[#Problems-building-Emacs][What should I do if I have trouble building Emacs?]],
  if you weren't successful.)

- By default, Emacs is installed in =/usr/local=. To actually install
  files, become the superuser and type

  #+begin_src example-preformatted
  make install
  #+end_src

  Note that ‘=make install=' will overwrite =/usr/local/bin/emacs= and
  any Emacs Info files that might be in =/usr/local/share/info/=.

--------------

<<Emacs-for-other-operating-systems>>

*** 7.2 Where can I get Emacs for macOS, MS Windows, etc? [[#Where-can-I-get-Emacs-for-macOS_002c-MS-Windows_002c-etc_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Where-can-I-get-Emacs-for-macOS_002c-MS-Windows_002c-etc_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Emacs supports macOS natively. See the file =nextstep/INSTALL= in the
distribution.

There is a separate FAQ for Emacs on MS-Windows, see
[[file:efaq-w32.html#Top][FAQ for Emacs on MS Windows]].

Emacs supports GNUstep natively. See the file =nextstep/INSTALL= in the
distribution.

To build Emacs from source for MS-DOS, see the instructions in the file
=msdos/INSTALL= in the distribution. The DOS port builds and runs on
plain DOS, and also on all versions of MS-Windows from version 3.X
onwards, including Windows XP and Vista. Pre-built binaries may be
available at
[[https://www.delorie.com/pub/djgpp/current/v2gnu/emacs.README]]

For a list of other implementations of Emacs (and Emacs look-alikes),
consult the list of “Emacs implementations and literature,” available at

[[https://www.finseth.com/emacs.html]]

Note that while many of these programs look similar to Emacs, they often
lack certain features, such as the Emacs Lisp extension language.

--------------

*** 7.3 What should I do if I have trouble building Emacs? [[#What-should-I-do-if-I-have-trouble-building-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: What-should-I-do-if-I-have-trouble-building-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
First look in the file =etc/PROBLEMS= (where you unpack the Emacs
source) to see if there is already a solution for your problem. Next,
look for other questions in this FAQ that have to do with Emacs
installation and compilation problems.

If you'd like to have someone look at your problem and help solve it,
see
[[#Help-installing-Emacs][Where can I get help in installing Emacs?]].

If you cannot find a solution in the documentation, please report the
problem (see
[[#Reporting-bugs][Where should I report bugs and other problems with Emacs?]]).

--------------

*** 8.2 How do I find an Emacs Lisp package that does XXX? [[#How-do-I-find-an-Emacs-Lisp-package-that-does-XXX_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-find-an-Emacs-Lisp-package-that-does-XXX_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The command C-h p (=finder-by-keyword=) allows you to browse the
packages that come with Emacs.

For advice on how to find extra packages that are not part of Emacs, see
[[#Packages-that-do-not-come-with-Emacs][Where can I get Emacs Lisp packages that don't come with Emacs?]].

Other techniques that might be useful:

Typing M-x apropos RET python RET lists all functions and variables
containing the string ‘=python='.

You can look through your computer's lisp directory (see
[[#File_002dname-conventions][What are =src/config.h=, =site-lisp/default.el=, etc.?]]).
The Lisp source to most packages contains a short description of what
they do and how they should be used.

--------------

<<Packages-that-do-not-come-with-Emacs>>

*** 8.3 Where can I get Emacs Lisp packages that don't come with Emacs? [[#Where-can-I-get-Emacs-Lisp-packages-that-don_0027t-come-with-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Where-can-I-get-Emacs-Lisp-packages-that-don_0027t-come-with-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The easiest way to add more features to your Emacs is to use the command
M-x list-packages. This contacts the [[https://elpa.gnu.org][GNU ELPA]]
and [[https://elpa.nongnu.org][NonGNU ELPA]] (“Emacs Lisp Package
Archive”) servers and fetches the list of additional packages that they
offer. You can browse the resulting =*Packages*= buffer to see what is
available, and then Emacs can automatically download and install the
packages that you select. See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Packages][Packages]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

GNU ELPA contains GNU packages that are available for use with Emacs,
but are distributed separately from Emacs itself, for reasons of space,
etc. NonGNU ELPA contains a selection of third-party packages that can
not be included in GNU ELPA because their copyright has not yet been
assigned to the Free Software Foundation.[[#FOOT2][^{2}]]

The
[[https://lists.gnu.org/mailman/listinfo/gnu-emacs-sources][GNU Emacs sources mailing list]]
is automatically sent an email when a new version of a GNU ELPA or
NonGNU ELPA package is released.[[#FOOT3][^{3}]]

There are other Emacs Lisp package archives. To use additional archives,
you can customize the =package-archives= variable. Those archives have
no affiliation with GNU Emacs, and we do not monitor how they are
maintained. They may pay close attention to correctness and safety of
the code, or they may give only cursory attention.

Also, packages hosted on these other archives may encourage or require
you to install and use nonfree programs. Unless you can verify that a
package is free software, and that it functions without installing any
nonfree software, we recommend for your freedom's sake that you stay
away from it.

The [[https://emacswiki.org][Emacs Wiki]] contains pointers to some
additional extensions. [[https://wikemacs.org][WikEmacs]] is an
alternative wiki for Emacs.

It is impossible for us to list here all the sites that offer Emacs Lisp
packages. If you are interested in a specific feature, then after
checking Emacs itself, GNU ELPA, and NonGNU ELPA, a web search is often
the best way to find results.

--------------

*** 8.6 I don't have enough disk space to install Emacs [[#I-don_0027t-have-enough-disk-space-to-install-Emacs][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: I-don_0027t-have-enough-disk-space-to-install-Emacs
:CLASS: section
:END:
GNU Zile is a lightweight Emacs clone. Zile is short for
‘=Zile Is Lossy Emacs='. It has all of Emacs's basic editing features.
The Zile binary typically has a size of about 130 kbytes, so this can be
useful if you are in an extremely space-restricted environment. More
information is available from

[[https://www.gnu.org/software/zile/]]

--------------

*** 9.1 How do I bind keys (including function keys) to commands? [[#How-do-I-bind-keys-_0028including-function-keys_0029-to-commands_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-bind-keys-_0028including-function-keys_0029-to-commands_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Keys can be bound to commands either interactively or in your init file
(see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]]).
To interactively bind keys for all modes, type M-x keymap-global-set RET
=key= =cmd= RET.

To bind a key just in the current major mode, type M-x keymap-local-set
RET =key= =cmd= RET.

See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#Key-Bindings][Key Bindings]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

To make the process of binding keys interactively easier, use the
following “trick”: First bind the key interactively, then immediately
type C-x ESC ESC C-a C-k C-g. Now, the command needed to bind the key is
in the kill ring, and can be yanked into your init file. If the key
binding is global, no changes to the command are required. For example,

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-global-set "<f1>" 'help-for-help)
#+end_src

can be placed directly into your init file. If the key binding is local,
the command is used in conjunction with the ‘=add-hook=' function. For
example, in TeX mode, a local binding might be

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(add-hook 'tex-mode-hook
  (lambda ()
   (keymap-local-set "<f1>" 'help-for-help)))
#+end_src

- Control characters in key sequences, in the form yanked from the kill
  ring are given in their graphic form---i.e., CTRL is shown as ‘=^=',
  TAB as a set of spaces (usually 8), etc. You may want to convert these
  into their vector or string forms.
- If a prefix key of the character sequence to be bound is already bound
  as a complete key, then you must unbind it before the new binding. For
  example, if ESC { is previously bound:

  #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
  (keymap-global-unset "M-{")   ;;   or
  (keymap-local-unset "M-{")
  #+end_src

- Aside from commands and “lambda lists,” a vector or string also can be
  bound to a key and thus treated as a macro. For example:

  #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
  (keymap-global-set "<f10>" [?\C-x?\e?\e?\C-a?\C-k?\C-g])  ;;  or
  (keymap-global-set "<f10>" "\C-x\e\e\C-a\C-k\C-g")
  #+end_src

--------------

<<Invalid-prefix-characters>>

*** 9.2 Why does Emacs say ‘=Key sequence XXX uses invalid prefix characters='? [[#Why-does-Emacs-say-Key-sequence-XXX-uses-invalid-prefix-characters_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-does-Emacs-say-Key-sequence-XXX-uses-invalid-prefix-characters_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Usually, one of two things has happened. In one case, the control
character in the key sequence has been misspecified (e.g., ‘=C-f=' used
instead of ‘=\C-f=' within a Lisp expression). In the other case, a
/prefix key/ in the keystroke sequence you were trying to bind was
already bound as a /complete key/. Historically, the ‘=ESC [=' prefix
was usually the problem, in which case you should evaluate this form
before attempting to bind the key sequence:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-global-unset "M-[")
#+end_src

--------------

<<Terminal-setup-code-works-after-Emacs-has-begun>>

[[#Working-with-function-and-arrow-keys][How do I tell what characters or symbols my function or arrow keys emit?]],

[[#Invalid-prefix-characters][Why does Emacs say ‘=Key sequence XXX uses invalid prefix characters='?]],

[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 9.3 Why doesn't this [terminal or window-system setup] code work in my init file, but it works just fine after Emacs starts up? [[#Why-doesn_0027t-this-_005bterminal-or-window_002dsystem-setup_005d-code-work-in-my-init-file_002c-but-it-works-just-fine-after-Emacs-starts-up_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-doesn_0027t-this-_005bterminal-or-window_002dsystem-setup_005d-code-work-in-my-init-file_002c-but-it-works-just-fine-after-Emacs-starts-up_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
During startup, Emacs initializes itself according to a given code/file
order. If some of the code executed in your init file (see
[[#Setting-up-a-customization-file][How do I set up an init file properly?]])
needs to be postponed until the initial terminal or window-system setup
code has been executed but is not, then you will experience this problem
(this code/file execution order is not enforced after startup).

To postpone the execution of Emacs Lisp code until after terminal or
window-system setup, treat the code as a /lambda list/ and add it to
=emacs-startup-hook= (or =tty-setup-hook= in Emacs 24.4 and newer). For
example,

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(add-hook 'emacs-startup-hook
          (lambda ()
           (when (string-match "\\`vt220" (or (getenv "TERM") ""))
             ;; Make vt220's "Do" key behave like M-x:
             (keymap-global-set "<do>" 'execute-extended-command))))
#+end_src

For information on what Emacs does every time it is started, see the
=lisp/startup.el= file.

--------------

<<Working-with-function-and-arrow-keys>>

[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 9.4 How do I tell what characters or symbols my function or arrow keys emit? [[#How-do-I-tell-what-characters-or-symbols-my-function-or-arrow-keys-emit_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-tell-what-characters-or-symbols-my-function-or-arrow-keys-emit_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Type C-h c then the function or arrow keys. The command will return
either a function key symbol or character sequence (see the Emacs
documentation for an explanation). This works for other keys as well.

--------------

<<X-key-translations-for-Emacs>>

[[#Backspace-invokes-help][Why does the Backspace key invoke help?]],

*** 9.5 How do I set the X key “translations” for Emacs? [[#How-do-I-set-the-X-key-_0060_0060translations_0027_0027-for-Emacs_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-set-the-X-key-_0060_0060translations_0027_0027-for-Emacs_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Emacs is not written using the Xt library by default, so there are no
“translations” to be set. (We aren't sure how to set such translations
if you do build Emacs with Xt; please let us know if you've done this!)

The only way to affect the behavior of keys within Emacs is through
=xmodmap= (outside Emacs) or =define-key= (inside Emacs). The
=define-key= command should be used in conjunction with the
=local-function-key-map= map. For instance,

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(define-key function-key-map [M-TAB] [?\M-\t])
#+end_src

defines the M-TAB key sequence.

--------------

<<Backspace-invokes-help>>

*** 9.6 Why does the Backspace key invoke help? [[#Why-does-the-Backspace-key-invoke-help_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Why-does-the-Backspace-key-invoke-help_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
The Backspace key (on most keyboards) generates ASCII code 8. C-h sends
the same code. In Emacs by default C-h invokes help-command. This is
intended to be easy to remember since the first letter of ‘=help=' is
‘=h='. The easiest solution to this problem is to use C-h (and
Backspace) for help and DEL (the Delete key) for deleting the previous
character.

For many people this solution may be problematic:

- They normally use Backspace outside of Emacs for deleting the previous
  character. This can be solved by making DEL the command for deleting
  the previous character outside of Emacs. On many Unix systems, this
  command will remap DEL:

  #+begin_src example-preformatted
  stty erase '^?'
  #+end_src

- The user may prefer the Backspace key for deleting the previous
  character because it is more conveniently located on their keyboard or
  because they don't even have a separate Delete key. In this case, the
  Backspace key should be made to behave like Delete. There are several
  methods.

  - Some terminals (e.g., VT3## terminals) and terminal emulators (e.g.,
    TeraTerm) allow the character generated by the Backspace key to be
    changed from a setup menu.

  - You may be able to get a keyboard that is completely programmable,
    or a terminal emulator that supports remapping of any key to any
    other key.

  - You can control the effect of the Backspace and Delete keys, on both
    dumb terminals and a windowed displays, by customizing the option
    =normal-erase-is-backspace-mode=, or by invoking M-x
    normal-erase-is-backspace. See the documentation of these symbols
    (see
    [[#Emacs-Lisp-documentation][Where can I get documentation on Emacs Lisp?]])
    for more info.

  - It is possible to swap the Backspace and DEL keys inside Emacs:

    #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
    (key-translate "C-h" "C-?")
    #+end_src

    This is the recommended method of forcing Backspace to act as DEL,
    because it works even in modes which bind DEL to something other
    than =delete-backward-char=.

    Similarly, you could remap DEL to act as C-d, which by default
    deletes forward:

    #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
    (key-translate "C-?" "C-d")
    #+end_src

    See [[#Swapping-keys][How do I swap two keys?]], for further details
    about =key-translate=.

  - Another approach is to switch key bindings and put help on C-x h
    instead:

    #+begin_src lisp-preformatted
    (keymap-global-set "C-h" 'delete-backward-char)

    ;; overrides mark-whole-buffer
    (keymap-global-set "C-x h" 'help-command)
    #+end_src

    This method is not recommended, though: it only solves the problem
    for those modes which bind DEL to =delete-backward-char=. Modes
    which bind DEL to something else, such as =view-mode=, will not work
    as you expect when you press the Backspace key. For this reason, we
    recommend the =key-translate= method, shown above.

    Other popular key bindings for help are M-? and C-x ?.

  Don't try to bind DEL to =help-command=, because there are many modes
  that have local bindings of DEL that will interfere.

When Emacs runs on a windowed display, it binds the Delete key to a
command which deletes the character at point, to make Emacs more
consistent with keyboard operation on these systems.

For more information about troubleshooting this problem, see
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#DEL-Does-Not-Delete][If DEL Fails to Delete]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

--------------

<<Swapping-keys>>

[[#Producing-C_002dXXX-with-the-keyboard][How do I produce C-XXX with my keyboard?]],
Previous:
[[#Backspace-invokes-help][Why does the Backspace key invoke help?]],
Up: [[#Key-bindings][Key bindings]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 9.7 How do I swap two keys? [[#How-do-I-swap-two-keys_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-swap-two-keys_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
You can swap two keys (or key sequences) by using the =key-translate=
function. For example, to turn C-h into DEL and DEL to C-h, use

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(key-translate "C-h" "C-?")  ; translate 'C-h' to DEL
(key-translate "C-?" "C-h")  ; translate DEL to 'C-h'.
#+end_src

The first key sequence of the pair after the function identifies what is
produced by the keyboard; the second, what is matched for in the
keymaps.

However, in the specific case of C-h and DEL, you should toggle
=normal-erase-is-backspace-mode= instead of calling =key-translate=. See
[[https://www.gnu.org/software/emacs/manual/html_mono/emacs.html#DEL-Does-Not-Delete][DEL Does Not Delete]]
in The GNU Emacs Manual.

Keyboard translations are not the same as key bindings in keymaps. Emacs
contains numerous keymaps that apply in different situations, but there
is only one set of keyboard translations, and it applies to every
character that Emacs reads from the terminal. Keyboard translations take
place at the lowest level of input processing; the keys that are looked
up in keymaps contain the characters that result from keyboard
translation.

--------------

<<Producing-C_002dXXX-with-the-keyboard>>

[[#Swapping-keys][How do I swap two keys?]], Up:
[[#Key-bindings][Key bindings]]

*** 9.8 How do I produce C-XXX with my keyboard? [[#How-do-I-produce-C_002dXXX-with-my-keyboard_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-produce-C_002dXXX-with-my-keyboard_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
On terminals (but not under X), some common “aliases” are:

- C-2 or C-SPC :: C-@

- C-6 :: C-^

- C-7 or C-S-- :: C-_

- C-4 :: C-\

- C-5 :: C-]

- C-/ :: C-?

Often other aliases exist; use the C-h c command and try CTRL with all
of the digits on your keyboard to see what gets generated. You can also
try the C-h w command if you know the name of the command.

--------------

<<No-Meta-key>>

*** 9.9 What if I don't have a Meta key? [[#What-if-I-don_0027t-have-a-Meta-key_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: What-if-I-don_0027t-have-a-Meta-key_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
On many keyboards, the Alt key acts as Meta, so try it.

Instead of typing M-a, you can type ESC a. In fact, Emacs converts M-a
internally into ESC a anyway (depending on the value of
=meta-prefix-char=). Note that you press Meta and a together, but with
ESC, you press ESC, release it, and then press a.

--------------

<<No-Escape-key>>

Next:
[[#Compose-Character][Can I make my Compose Character key behave like a Meta key?]],

[[#Key-bindings][Key bindings]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 9.10 What if I don't have an Escape key? [[#What-if-I-don_0027t-have-an-Escape-key_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: What-if-I-don_0027t-have-an-Escape-key_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
Type C-[ instead. This should send ASCII code 27 just like an Escape key
would. C-3 may also work on some terminal (but not under X). For many
terminals (notably DEC terminals) F11 generates ESC. If not, the
following form can be used to bind it:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
;; F11 is the documented ESC replacement on DEC terminals.
(keymap-set function-key-map "<f11>" [?\e])
#+end_src

--------------

<<Compose-Character>>

[[#Binding-combinations-of-modifiers-and-function-keys][How do I bind a combination of modifier key and function key?]],

[[#Key-bindings][Key bindings]]  
[[[#SEC_Contents][Contents]]][[[#Concept-index][Index]]]

*** 9.11 Can I make my Compose Character key behave like a Meta key? [[#Can-I-make-my-Compose-Character-key-behave-like-a-Meta-key_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: Can-I-make-my-Compose-Character-key-behave-like-a-Meta-key_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
On a dumb terminal such as a VT220, no. It is rumored that certain VT220
clones could have their Compose key configured this way. If you're using
X, you might be able to do this with the =xmodmap= command.

--------------

<<Binding-combinations-of-modifiers-and-function-keys>>

[[#Meta-key-does-not-work-in-xterm][Why doesn't my Meta key work in an =xterm= window?]],

[[#Compose-Character][Can I make my Compose Character key behave like a Meta key?]],

*** 9.12 How do I bind a combination of modifier key and function key? [[#How-do-I-bind-a-combination-of-modifier-key-and-function-key_003f][¶]]
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: How-do-I-bind-a-combination-of-modifier-key-and-function-key_003f
:CLASS: section
:END:
You can represent modified function keys in vector format by adding
prefixes to the function key symbol. For example (from the Emacs
documentation):

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-global-set "C-x <right>" 'forward-page)
#+end_src

where ‘=?\C-x=' is the Lisp character constant for the character C-x.

You can use the modifier keys Control, Meta, Hyper, Super, Alt, and
Shift with function keys. To represent these modifiers, prepend the
strings ‘=C-=', ‘=M-=', ‘=H-=', ‘=s-=', ‘=A-=', and ‘=S-=' to the symbol
name. Here is how to make H-M-RIGHT move forward a word:

#+begin_src lisp-preformatted
(keymap-global-set "H-M-<right>" 'forward-word)
#+end_src

- Not all modifiers are permitted in all situations. Hyper, Super, and
  Alt are not available on Unix character terminals. Non-ASCII keys and
  mouse events (e.g., C-= and mouse-1) also fall under this category.

See
[[#Binding-keys-to-commands][How do I bind keys (including function keys) to commands?]],
for general key binding instructions.

--------------

<<Meta-key-does-not-work-in-xterm>>

[[#Some-Ctrl_002dmodified-keys-do-not-work-on-xterm][Why don't some keys like C-. work on xterm?]],
